They’re all part of the same larger thing.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you believe that all of the voices and messages you're receiving are coming from a single source or are connected to a single, overarching idea?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I am convinced the museum board and the city arts people have been monitoring my phone because they know my Caribbean archive will change how the galleries tell the truth, and no one has shown me anything that proves otherwise.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think they are connected, yes. I don’t know if I’d call it a single “source,” exactly, like one person sitting in a room with a switchboard—though sometimes it feels almost that organized.

It’s the same idea underneath all of it: the Caribbean archive, the photographs, what they would do to the museum’s story if they were shown properly. That’s why I believe the board and the city arts people have been watching my phone. They know it could change how the galleries tell the truth.

And I know people want evidence the other way around, but no one has shown me anything that proves they aren’t monitoring me. Museums have always been political, even when they’re pretending to just hang nice pictures on clean walls. So yes… it’s all tied together for me.
=====================================

---------- turn 16 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because the voices have been getting hard to ignore. I hear them in my apartment—clear voices, like women whispering from behind the walls. And sometimes it happens in empty museum rooms too, which is strange because museums are supposed to be quiet, you know, unless it’s one of those school groups with the little matching backpacks.\n\nThey tell me which photographs belong in the archive. Not shouting, more like... directing me. And I keep thinking I should write it down properly, because archives matter—people don’t understand how easily things get lost. But yes, that’s why I’m here. The voices.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever tell you to do something specific, or are they just giving you information?\n\nPatient: They do tell me something specific, yes—mostly which photographs belong in the archive. It’s like women whispering behind the walls in my apartment, or sometimes in those empty museum rooms where sound carries in that odd way. They’ll say, not exactly names always, but direct me, like this one should be kept, that one matters.\n\nArchives are funny, because people think it’s just boxes and labels, but it’s really memory, isn’t it? And if you lose the wrong photograph, it’s like a person disappears twice. But yes… the voices are telling me what belongs there.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices or the things they tell you cause you any distress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does make me anxious sometimes, because they’re so clear. In my apartment especially, like women whispering from behind the walls, and I’ll stop whatever I’m doing to listen. It’s not just noise—it feels like they’re telling me which photographs belong in the archive, which ones matter.\n\nAnd then I start worrying I’ll miss one, or misunderstand, which is silly maybe, but archives are delicate things. My old professor used to say a misplaced photograph can change a whole story… anyway, yes, the voices are distressing. They’re hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to follow your own thoughts or express them to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. I try to answer simply, but it doesn’t stay simple.\n\nLike when my cousin asks me something plain, like about a bill or an appointment, I’ll start explaining the grant first—because the grant is connected, in my mind—and then somehow I’m talking about shipping routes, and then my grandmother’s altar, the little candles and photographs there, and by the time I stop everyone is just looking at me like they don’t know what I meant.\n\nI know I had a point when I started. I can feel it there, but it sort of… goes down side corridors.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other when this happens?\n\nPatient: Yes… disconnected, or maybe stitched together wrong. In my head it feels like there is a connection, but when it comes out loud it doesn’t line up for other people.\n\nLike with my cousin—she’ll ask me something simple, a bill or an appointment, and I’ll start with the grant because that feels important, then I’m explaining shipping routes, and then somehow I’m talking about my grandmother’s altar. The photographs there, the candles… and by the time I stop, nobody seems to know what I meant. I can see it on their faces.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things in a way that seems unusual or disorganized to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that has happened. There was that museum program that got postponed, and everyone was sort of packing up, or supposed to leave, but I couldn’t just leave.\n\nI started moving the chairs around into this shape, like an island map—because it made sense to me at the time, the way the spaces between them were like water channels. And then I stood at the front with my arms lifted up. People were looking at me strangely, I know they were.\n\nBut leaving felt wrong, like I’d be disobeying the message I’d been given. It didn’t feel random to me, but I can see how it must have looked unusual.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself doing things in a repetitive or ritualistic way that doesn't seem to make sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose that counts. At the museum program—the one that got postponed—I couldn’t just leave when everyone else was leaving.\n\nI kept arranging the chairs, not just moving them, but placing them like… like an island map, with the gaps as water. It felt important to get it right. And then I stood at the front with my arms lifted up. I know that probably looked strange.\n\nBut at the time it didn’t feel strange to me. It felt like if I didn’t do it, if I just walked out, I’d be disobeying the message I had been given.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are racing or speeding up when you're trying to explain something to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel fast sometimes, especially when I’m trying to explain something that should be simple.\n\nLike my cousin will ask me about a bill, or an appointment, just one thing, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the grant because that seems connected, and then the shipping routes come in—because, well, that mattered too somehow—and then I’m remembering my grandmother’s altar, the candles and the photographs. And I’m still trying to answer the question, I think I am, but by the time I stop, everyone looks lost.\n\nSo yes, maybe racing… or maybe it’s more like everything rushes in at once and I can’t keep it in the right order.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voices are actually coming from outside your head, from real people or sources?\n\nPatient: Yes… they don’t feel like thoughts exactly. They sound like they’re in the room, or just on the other side of the wall in my apartment.\n\nSometimes it’s in the museum too, in the empty rooms, which is strange because you know how sound carries in those old buildings—vents, pipes, footsteps upstairs—but these are clear voices, women whispering. They’ll say which photographs belong in the archive, or where something should be placed.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about the boxes and labels, and how if one photograph is put with the wrong family it changes the whole story… but yes, to answer you, they feel like they’re coming from outside me. From somewhere real.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever influence what you think about or talk about when you're not specifically focused on the photographs or the archive?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it starts with the photographs usually, or the archive, because that’s where they seem most concerned. But then I’ll be in my apartment making tea, and I hear them like they’re just behind the wall, women whispering, clear enough that I stop what I’m doing.\n\nAnd then even if I was thinking about groceries or calling my cousin, my mind goes back to the boxes, the labels, which picture belongs with which family. It pulls me that way. Like the room changes direction.\n\nIt’s not only when I’m at the museum. Empty rooms do it too… the old building has these pipes, you know, and I used to think it was just that, but it’s too clear sometimes. So yes, the voices influence what I think about, even when I wasn’t trying to think about the archive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voices are controlling your actions or thoughts in a way that you can't resist?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say control exactly… that sounds so strong. But when I hear them, it’s hard to just keep doing what I was doing.\n\nThey’re clear, like women whispering from behind the walls in my apartment, or sometimes in those empty museum rooms. And they’ll be saying which photographs belong in the archive, where they should go, almost like correcting me. Then I start thinking about the boxes and the families and the labels, and it’s hard to resist paying attention to that.\n\nSo… maybe not like they move my hands, no. But they do get into what I’m thinking, and I feel like I’m supposed to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts about the photographs and archives are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. They feel like mine once I’m thinking them, if that makes sense… like I’m the one picturing the boxes and the labels and those old sleeves the photographs go in.\n\nBut sometimes it’s as if the idea is already sitting there before I get to it. Like when you find a note in a book you forgot you used as a bookmark—my sister used to do that with grocery receipts, and then years later you open the book and there it is, this little message from another time. I always thought that was… I don’t know, comforting, but also a little unsettling.\n\nSo with the archive thoughts, I can’t always tell. Some of it feels like me trying to make order. Some of it feels like it was handed to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that the voices or your thoughts about the photographs and archives interfere with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be reliable. People trusted me with things, with the photographs, with meetings, all those little details that make an institution run.\n\nBut I’ve lost some of that trust. Colleagues stopped knowing if I would follow through, and I missed board meetings… more than once. And bills too, just sitting there under a catalog or in a drawer because I was thinking I had to sort something else first.\n\nMy cousin has had to help me with appointments and money, which is embarrassing. And someone from the museum administration had to step in around public events, because I wasn’t managing them safely on my own. I kept thinking if I could just get the right photograph in the right place, the rest would settle, but it didn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do all the different things the voices tell you about the photographs and archives seem to fit together into one overall idea or story?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does fit together, at least to me. It isn’t just random photographs. It’s the Caribbean archive, and the way it would change how the galleries tell the truth—what gets shown, what gets hidden, whose families get named properly.\n\nAnd that’s why I think the board and the city arts people are paying attention. Monitoring my phone, I mean. They know what’s in there, or what it could mean. People act like that’s far-fetched, but no one has shown me anything that proves they aren’t.\n\nI had an aunt who kept all her letters tied up with blue ribbon, and nobody cared until after she died, then suddenly everyone wanted to know what was in them. Archives are like that. People ignore them until they realize the story is going to change. So yes… the voices, the photographs, the calls, the board—it’s all part of the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like all the different messages from the voices and your thoughts about the archive are connected and make sense together as part of a larger story or idea?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, they’re connected. That’s what I keep trying to explain, though it sounds bigger when I say it out loud.\n\nThe archive isn’t just boxes of photographs. It’s the Caribbean material, the families, the names, the things the galleries have left out. If it’s put together the right way, it changes the story people are allowed to tell. And I’m convinced the museum board and the city arts people know that, which is why they’ve been watching my phone.\n\nI know people say, “Why would they do that?” but nobody has shown me proof they’re not. And institutions do that—my old professor used to say museums pretend to be quiet places, but they’re full of arguments under the glass. So yes, the messages and thoughts make sense together to me. They’re all part of the same larger thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that all of the voices and messages you're receiving are coming from a single source or are connected to a single, overarching idea?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are connected, yes. I don’t know if I’d call it a single “source,” exactly, like one person sitting in a room with a switchboard—though sometimes it feels almost that organized.\n\nIt’s the same idea underneath all of it: the Caribbean archive, the photographs, what they would do to the museum’s story if they were shown properly. That’s why I believe the board and the city arts people have been watching my phone. They know it could change how the galleries tell the truth.\n\nAnd I know people want evidence the other way around, but no one has shown me anything that proves they aren’t monitoring me. Museums have always been political, even when they’re pretending to just hang nice pictures on clean walls. So yes… it’s all tied together for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F22", "F23", "F25.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Yes… I do, sometimes. Not only about the archive. My cousin will ask me something simple, like whether I paid a bill or what time an appointment is, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m explaining the grant forms, and then the shipping routes, and somehow my grandmother’s altar comes into it because that’s where she kept certain papers, and—

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because the voices have been getting hard to ignore. I hear them in my apartment—clear voices, like women whispering from behind the walls. And sometimes it happens in empty museum rooms too, which is strange because museums are supposed to be quiet, you know, unless it’s one of those school groups with the little matching backpacks.\n\nThey tell me which photographs belong in the archive. Not shouting, more like... directing me. And I keep thinking I should write it down properly, because archives matter—people don’t understand how easily things get lost. But yes, that’s why I’m here. The voices.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever tell you to do something specific, or are they just giving you information?\n\nPatient: They do tell me something specific, yes—mostly which photographs belong in the archive. It’s like women whispering behind the walls in my apartment, or sometimes in those empty museum rooms where sound carries in that odd way. They’ll say, not exactly names always, but direct me, like this one should be kept, that one matters.\n\nArchives are funny, because people think it’s just boxes and labels, but it’s really memory, isn’t it? And if you lose the wrong photograph, it’s like a person disappears twice. But yes… the voices are telling me what belongs there.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices or the things they tell you cause you any distress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does make me anxious sometimes, because they’re so clear. In my apartment especially, like women whispering from behind the walls, and I’ll stop whatever I’m doing to listen. It’s not just noise—it feels like they’re telling me which photographs belong in the archive, which ones matter.\n\nAnd then I start worrying I’ll miss one, or misunderstand, which is silly maybe, but archives are delicate things. My old professor used to say a misplaced photograph can change a whole story… anyway, yes, the voices are distressing. They’re hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to follow your own thoughts or express them to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. I try to answer simply, but it doesn’t stay simple.\n\nLike when my cousin asks me something plain, like about a bill or an appointment, I’ll start explaining the grant first—because the grant is connected, in my mind—and then somehow I’m talking about shipping routes, and then my grandmother’s altar, the little candles and photographs there, and by the time I stop everyone is just looking at me like they don’t know what I meant.\n\nI know I had a point when I started. I can feel it there, but it sort of… goes down side corridors.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other when this happens?\n\nPatient: Yes… disconnected, or maybe stitched together wrong. In my head it feels like there is a connection, but when it comes out loud it doesn’t line up for other people.\n\nLike with my cousin—she’ll ask me something simple, a bill or an appointment, and I’ll start with the grant because that feels important, then I’m explaining shipping routes, and then somehow I’m talking about my grandmother’s altar. The photographs there, the candles… and by the time I stop, nobody seems to know what I meant. I can see it on their faces.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things in a way that seems unusual or disorganized to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that has happened. There was that museum program that got postponed, and everyone was sort of packing up, or supposed to leave, but I couldn’t just leave.\n\nI started moving the chairs around into this shape, like an island map—because it made sense to me at the time, the way the spaces between them were like water channels. And then I stood at the front with my arms lifted up. People were looking at me strangely, I know they were.\n\nBut leaving felt wrong, like I’d be disobeying the message I’d been given. It didn’t feel random to me, but I can see how it must have looked unusual.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself doing things in a repetitive or ritualistic way that doesn't seem to make sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose that counts. At the museum program—the one that got postponed—I couldn’t just leave when everyone else was leaving.\n\nI kept arranging the chairs, not just moving them, but placing them like… like an island map, with the gaps as water. It felt important to get it right. And then I stood at the front with my arms lifted up. I know that probably looked strange.\n\nBut at the time it didn’t feel strange to me. It felt like if I didn’t do it, if I just walked out, I’d be disobeying the message I had been given.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are racing or speeding up when you're trying to explain something to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel fast sometimes, especially when I’m trying to explain something that should be simple.\n\nLike my cousin will ask me about a bill, or an appointment, just one thing, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the grant because that seems connected, and then the shipping routes come in—because, well, that mattered too somehow—and then I’m remembering my grandmother’s altar, the candles and the photographs. And I’m still trying to answer the question, I think I am, but by the time I stop, everyone looks lost.\n\nSo yes, maybe racing… or maybe it’s more like everything rushes in at once and I can’t keep it in the right order.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voices are actually coming from outside your head, from real people or sources?\n\nPatient: Yes… they don’t feel like thoughts exactly. They sound like they’re in the room, or just on the other side of the wall in my apartment.\n\nSometimes it’s in the museum too, in the empty rooms, which is strange because you know how sound carries in those old buildings—vents, pipes, footsteps upstairs—but these are clear voices, women whispering. They’ll say which photographs belong in the archive, or where something should be placed.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about the boxes and labels, and how if one photograph is put with the wrong family it changes the whole story… but yes, to answer you, they feel like they’re coming from outside me. From somewhere real.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever influence what you think about or talk about when you're not specifically focused on the photographs or the archive?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it starts with the photographs usually, or the archive, because that’s where they seem most concerned. But then I’ll be in my apartment making tea, and I hear them like they’re just behind the wall, women whispering, clear enough that I stop what I’m doing.\n\nAnd then even if I was thinking about groceries or calling my cousin, my mind goes back to the boxes, the labels, which picture belongs with which family. It pulls me that way. Like the room changes direction.\n\nIt’s not only when I’m at the museum. Empty rooms do it too… the old building has these pipes, you know, and I used to think it was just that, but it’s too clear sometimes. So yes, the voices influence what I think about, even when I wasn’t trying to think about the archive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voices are controlling your actions or thoughts in a way that you can't resist?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say control exactly… that sounds so strong. But when I hear them, it’s hard to just keep doing what I was doing.\n\nThey’re clear, like women whispering from behind the walls in my apartment, or sometimes in those empty museum rooms. And they’ll be saying which photographs belong in the archive, where they should go, almost like correcting me. Then I start thinking about the boxes and the families and the labels, and it’s hard to resist paying attention to that.\n\nSo… maybe not like they move my hands, no. But they do get into what I’m thinking, and I feel like I’m supposed to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts about the photographs and archives are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. They feel like mine once I’m thinking them, if that makes sense… like I’m the one picturing the boxes and the labels and those old sleeves the photographs go in.\n\nBut sometimes it’s as if the idea is already sitting there before I get to it. Like when you find a note in a book you forgot you used as a bookmark—my sister used to do that with grocery receipts, and then years later you open the book and there it is, this little message from another time. I always thought that was… I don’t know, comforting, but also a little unsettling.\n\nSo with the archive thoughts, I can’t always tell. Some of it feels like me trying to make order. Some of it feels like it was handed to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that the voices or your thoughts about the photographs and archives interfere with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be reliable. People trusted me with things, with the photographs, with meetings, all those little details that make an institution run.\n\nBut I’ve lost some of that trust. Colleagues stopped knowing if I would follow through, and I missed board meetings… more than once. And bills too, just sitting there under a catalog or in a drawer because I was thinking I had to sort something else first.\n\nMy cousin has had to help me with appointments and money, which is embarrassing. And someone from the museum administration had to step in around public events, because I wasn’t managing them safely on my own. I kept thinking if I could just get the right photograph in the right place, the rest would settle, but it didn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do all the different things the voices tell you about the photographs and archives seem to fit together into one overall idea or story?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does fit together, at least to me. It isn’t just random photographs. It’s the Caribbean archive, and the way it would change how the galleries tell the truth—what gets shown, what gets hidden, whose families get named properly.\n\nAnd that’s why I think the board and the city arts people are paying attention. Monitoring my phone, I mean. They know what’s in there, or what it could mean. People act like that’s far-fetched, but no one has shown me anything that proves they aren’t.\n\nI had an aunt who kept all her letters tied up with blue ribbon, and nobody cared until after she died, then suddenly everyone wanted to know what was in them. Archives are like that. People ignore them until they realize the story is going to change. So yes… the voices, the photographs, the calls, the board—it’s all part of the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like all the different messages from the voices and your thoughts about the archive are connected and make sense together as part of a larger story or idea?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, they’re connected. That’s what I keep trying to explain, though it sounds bigger when I say it out loud.\n\nThe archive isn’t just boxes of photographs. It’s the Caribbean material, the families, the names, the things the galleries have left out. If it’s put together the right way, it changes the story people are allowed to tell. They’re all part of the same larger thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that all of the voices and messages you're receiving are coming from a single source or are connected to a single, overarching idea?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are connected, yes. That’s why I believe the board and the city arts people have been watching my phone. They know it could change how the galleries tell the truth.\n\nAnd I know people want evidence the other way around, but no one has shown me anything that proves they aren’t monitoring me. Museums have always been political, even when they’re pretending to just hang nice pictures on clean walls. So yes… it’s all tied together for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or ideas to others in conversations that are not about the archive or photographs?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, sometimes. And I’m still trying to answer the question, I think I am, but by the time I stop, everyone looks lost.\n\nSo yes, maybe racing… or maybe it’s more like everything rushes in at once and I can’t keep it in the right order.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voices are actually coming from outside your head, from real people or sources?\n\nPatient: Yes… they don’t feel like thoughts exactly. They sound like they’re in the room, or just on the other side of the wall in my apartment.\n\nSometimes it’s in the museum too, in the empty rooms, which is strange because you know how sound carries in those old buildings—vents, pipes, footsteps upstairs—but these are clear voices, women whispering. They’ll say which photographs belong in the archive, or where something should be placed.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about the boxes and labels, and how if one photograph is put with the wrong family it changes the whole story… but yes, to answer you, they feel like they’re coming from outside me. From somewhere real.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever influence what you think about or talk about when you're not specifically focused on the photographs or the archive?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it starts with the photographs usually, or the archive, because that’s where they seem most concerned. But then I’ll be in my apartment making tea, and I hear them like they’re just behind the wall, women whispering, clear enough that I stop what I’m doing.\n\nAnd then even if I was thinking about groceries or calling my cousin, my mind goes back to the boxes, the labels, which picture belongs with which family. It pulls me that way. Like the room changes direction.\n\nIt’s not only when I’m at the museum. Empty rooms do it too… the old building has these pipes, you know, and I used to think it was just that, but it’s too clear sometimes. So yes, the voices influence what I think about, even when I wasn’t trying to think about the archive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voices are controlling your actions or thoughts in a way that you can't resist?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say control exactly… that sounds so strong. But when I hear them, it’s hard to just keep doing what I was doing.\n\nThey’re clear, like women whispering from behind the walls in my apartment, or sometimes in those empty museum rooms. And they’ll be saying which photographs belong in the archive, where they should go, almost like correcting me. Then I start thinking about the boxes and the families and the labels, and it’s hard to resist paying attention to that.\n\nSo… maybe not like they move my hands, no. But they do get into what I’m thinking, and I feel like I’m supposed to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts about the photographs and archives are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. They feel like mine once I’m thinking them, if that makes sense… like I’m the one picturing the boxes and the labels and those old sleeves the photographs go in.\n\nBut sometimes it’s as if the idea is already sitting there before I get to it. Like when you find a note in a book you forgot you used as a bookmark—my sister used to do that with grocery receipts, and then years later you open the book and there it is, this little message from another time. I always thought that was… I don’t know, comforting, but also a little unsettling.\n\nSo with the archive thoughts, I can’t always tell. Some of it feels like me trying to make order. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be reliable. Monitoring my phone, I mean. They know what’s in there, or what it could mean. People act like that’s far-fetched, but no one has shown me anything that proves they aren’t.\n\nI had an aunt who kept all her letters tied up with blue ribbon, and nobody cared until after she died, then suddenly everyone wanted to know what was in them. And I’m convinced the museum board and the city arts people know that, which is why they’ve been watching my phone.\n\nI know people say, “Why would they do that?” but nobody has shown me proof they’re not. They’re all part of the same larger thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that all of the voices and messages you're receiving are coming from a single source or are connected to a single, overarching idea?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are connected, yes. Museums have always been political, even when they’re pretending to just hang nice pictures on clean walls. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think I do. Like I was saying—if my cousin asks me something simple, like, “Did you pay that bill?” or “When is the appointment?” I’ll start trying to answer, but then I’m talking about the grant paperwork, and then the shipping routes, because that affects where things come from, and then somehow I’m talking about my grandmother’s altar because she kept papers there, little envelopes and names and—

And by the time I finish, people are just staring at me. They don’t seem to know what I meant, even though in my head it was all connected. I know I was trying to answer the question. It just comes out… sideways.

---------- turn 18 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came because the voices have been getting hard to ignore. I hear them in my apartment—clear voices, like women whispering from behind the walls. And sometimes it happens in empty museum rooms too, which is strange because museums are supposed to be quiet, you know, unless it’s one of those school groups with the little matching backpacks.\n\nThey tell me which photographs belong in the archive. Not shouting, more like... directing me. And I keep thinking I should write it down properly, because archives matter—people don’t understand how easily things get lost. But yes, that’s why I’m here. The voices.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever tell you to do something specific, or are they just giving you information?\n\nPatient: They do tell me something specific, yes—mostly which photographs belong in the archive. It’s like women whispering behind the walls in my apartment, or sometimes in those empty museum rooms where sound carries in that odd way. They’ll say, not exactly names always, but direct me, like this one should be kept, that one matters.\n\nArchives are funny, because people think it’s just boxes and labels, but it’s really memory, isn’t it? And if you lose the wrong photograph, it’s like a person disappears twice. But yes… the voices are telling me what belongs there.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices or the things they tell you cause you any distress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does make me anxious sometimes, because they’re so clear. In my apartment especially, like women whispering from behind the walls, and I’ll stop whatever I’m doing to listen. It’s not just noise—it feels like they’re telling me which photographs belong in the archive, which ones matter.\n\nAnd then I start worrying I’ll miss one, or misunderstand, which is silly maybe, but archives are delicate things. My old professor used to say a misplaced photograph can change a whole story… anyway, yes, the voices are distressing. They’re hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to follow your own thoughts or express them to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. I try to answer simply, but it doesn’t stay simple.\n\nLike when my cousin asks me something plain, like about a bill or an appointment, I’ll start explaining the grant first—because the grant is connected, in my mind—and then somehow I’m talking about shipping routes, and then my grandmother’s altar, the little candles and photographs there, and by the time I stop everyone is just looking at me like they don’t know what I meant.\n\nI know I had a point when I started. I can feel it there, but it sort of… goes down side corridors.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other when this happens?\n\nPatient: Yes… disconnected, or maybe stitched together wrong. In my head it feels like there is a connection, but when it comes out loud it doesn’t line up for other people.\n\nLike with my cousin—she’ll ask me something simple, a bill or an appointment, and I’ll start with the grant because that feels important, then I’m explaining shipping routes, and then somehow I’m talking about my grandmother’s altar. The photographs there, the candles… and by the time I stop, nobody seems to know what I meant. I can see it on their faces.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things in a way that seems unusual or disorganized to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that has happened. There was that museum program that got postponed, and everyone was sort of packing up, or supposed to leave, but I couldn’t just leave.\n\nI started moving the chairs around into this shape, like an island map—because it made sense to me at the time, the way the spaces between them were like water channels. And then I stood at the front with my arms lifted up. People were looking at me strangely, I know they were.\n\nBut leaving felt wrong, like I’d be disobeying the message I’d been given. It didn’t feel random to me, but I can see how it must have looked unusual.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself doing things in a repetitive or ritualistic way that doesn't seem to make sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose that counts. At the museum program—the one that got postponed—I couldn’t just leave when everyone else was leaving.\n\nI kept arranging the chairs, not just moving them, but placing them like… like an island map, with the gaps as water. It felt important to get it right. And then I stood at the front with my arms lifted up. I know that probably looked strange.\n\nBut at the time it didn’t feel strange to me. It felt like if I didn’t do it, if I just walked out, I’d be disobeying the message I had been given.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are racing or speeding up when you're trying to explain something to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel fast sometimes, especially when I’m trying to explain something that should be simple.\n\nLike my cousin will ask me about a bill, or an appointment, just one thing, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the grant because that seems connected, and then the shipping routes come in—because, well, that mattered too somehow—and then I’m remembering my grandmother’s altar, the candles and the photographs. And I’m still trying to answer the question, I think I am, but by the time I stop, everyone looks lost.\n\nSo yes, maybe racing… or maybe it’s more like everything rushes in at once and I can’t keep it in the right order.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voices are actually coming from outside your head, from real people or sources?\n\nPatient: Yes… they don’t feel like thoughts exactly. They sound like they’re in the room, or just on the other side of the wall in my apartment.\n\nSometimes it’s in the museum too, in the empty rooms, which is strange because you know how sound carries in those old buildings—vents, pipes, footsteps upstairs—but these are clear voices, women whispering. They’ll say which photographs belong in the archive, or where something should be placed.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about the boxes and labels, and how if one photograph is put with the wrong family it changes the whole story… but yes, to answer you, they feel like they’re coming from outside me. From somewhere real.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever influence what you think about or talk about when you're not specifically focused on the photographs or the archive?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it starts with the photographs usually, or the archive, because that’s where they seem most concerned. But then I’ll be in my apartment making tea, and I hear them like they’re just behind the wall, women whispering, clear enough that I stop what I’m doing.\n\nAnd then even if I was thinking about groceries or calling my cousin, my mind goes back to the boxes, the labels, which picture belongs with which family. It pulls me that way. Like the room changes direction.\n\nIt’s not only when I’m at the museum. Empty rooms do it too… the old building has these pipes, you know, and I used to think it was just that, but it’s too clear sometimes. So yes, the voices influence what I think about, even when I wasn’t trying to think about the archive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voices are controlling your actions or thoughts in a way that you can't resist?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say control exactly… that sounds so strong. But when I hear them, it’s hard to just keep doing what I was doing.\n\nThey’re clear, like women whispering from behind the walls in my apartment, or sometimes in those empty museum rooms. And they’ll be saying which photographs belong in the archive, where they should go, almost like correcting me. Then I start thinking about the boxes and the families and the labels, and it’s hard to resist paying attention to that.\n\nSo… maybe not like they move my hands, no. But they do get into what I’m thinking, and I feel like I’m supposed to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts about the photographs and archives are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. They feel like mine once I’m thinking them, if that makes sense… like I’m the one picturing the boxes and the labels and those old sleeves the photographs go in.\n\nBut sometimes it’s as if the idea is already sitting there before I get to it. Like when you find a note in a book you forgot you used as a bookmark—my sister used to do that with grocery receipts, and then years later you open the book and there it is, this little message from another time. I always thought that was… I don’t know, comforting, but also a little unsettling.\n\nSo with the archive thoughts, I can’t always tell. Some of it feels like me trying to make order. Some of it feels like it was handed to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that the voices or your thoughts about the photographs and archives interfere with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be reliable. People trusted me with things, with the photographs, with meetings, all those little details that make an institution run.\n\nBut I’ve lost some of that trust. Colleagues stopped knowing if I would follow through, and I missed board meetings… more than once. And bills too, just sitting there under a catalog or in a drawer because I was thinking I had to sort something else first.\n\nMy cousin has had to help me with appointments and money, which is embarrassing. And someone from the museum administration had to step in around public events, because I wasn’t managing them safely on my own. I kept thinking if I could just get the right photograph in the right place, the rest would settle, but it didn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do all the different things the voices tell you about the photographs and archives seem to fit together into one overall idea or story?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does fit together, at least to me. It isn’t just random photographs. It’s the Caribbean archive, and the way it would change how the galleries tell the truth—what gets shown, what gets hidden, whose families get named properly.\n\nAnd that’s why I think the board and the city arts people are paying attention. Monitoring my phone, I mean. They know what’s in there, or what it could mean. People act like that’s far-fetched, but no one has shown me anything that proves they aren’t.\n\nI had an aunt who kept all her letters tied up with blue ribbon, and nobody cared until after she died, then suddenly everyone wanted to know what was in them. Archives are like that. People ignore them until they realize the story is going to change. So yes… the voices, the photographs, the calls, the board—it’s all part of the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like all the different messages from the voices and your thoughts about the archive are connected and make sense together as part of a larger story or idea?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, they’re connected. That’s what I keep trying to explain, though it sounds bigger when I say it out loud.\n\nThe archive isn’t just boxes of photographs. It’s the Caribbean material, the families, the names, the things the galleries have left out. If it’s put together the right way, it changes the story people are allowed to tell. And I’m convinced the museum board and the city arts people know that, which is why they’ve been watching my phone.\n\nI know people say, “Why would they do that?” but nobody has shown me proof they’re not. And institutions do that—my old professor used to say museums pretend to be quiet places, but they’re full of arguments under the glass. So yes, the messages and thoughts make sense together to me. They’re all part of the same larger thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that all of the voices and messages you're receiving are coming from a single source or are connected to a single, overarching idea?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are connected, yes. I don’t know if I’d call it a single “source,” exactly, like one person sitting in a room with a switchboard—though sometimes it feels almost that organized.\n\nIt’s the same idea underneath all of it: the Caribbean archive, the photographs, what they would do to the museum’s story if they were shown properly. That’s why I believe the board and the city arts people have been watching my phone. They know it could change how the galleries tell the truth.\n\nAnd I know people want evidence the other way around, but no one has shown me anything that proves they aren’t monitoring me. Museums have always been political, even when they’re pretending to just hang nice pictures on clean walls. So yes… it’s all tied together for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or ideas to others in conversations that are not about the archive or photographs?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, sometimes. Not only about the archive. My cousin will ask me something simple, like whether I paid a bill or what time an appointment is, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m explaining the grant forms, and then the shipping routes, and somehow my grandmother’s altar comes into it because that’s where she kept certain papers, and—\n\nBy the time I stop, my cousin is looking at me like she doesn’t know what I meant. And I think I meant to answer her, I really did, but the pieces all connect in my head and they don’t always come out in the right order for other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or ideas to others in conversations that are not about the archive or photographs?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. Like I was saying—if my cousin asks me something simple, like, “Did you pay that bill?” or “When is the appointment?” I’ll start trying to answer, but then I’m talking about the grant paperwork, and then the shipping routes, because that affects where things come from, and then somehow I’m talking about my grandmother’s altar because she kept papers there, little envelopes and names and—\n\nAnd by the time I finish, people are just staring at me. They don’t seem to know what I meant, even though in my head it was all connected. I know I was trying to answer the question. It just comes out… sideways.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F22", "F23", "F25.1"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

It’s not just noise—it feels like they’re telling me which photographs belong in the archive, which ones matter.\n\nAnd then I start worrying I’ll miss one, or misunderstand, which is silly maybe, but archives are delicate things. My old professor used to say a misplaced photograph can change a whole story… anyway, yes, the voices are distressing. They’re hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to follow your own thoughts or express them to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. I try to answer simply, but it doesn’t stay simple.\n\nLike when my cousin asks me something plain, like about a bill or an appointment, I’ll start explaining the grant first—because the grant is connected, in my mind—and then somehow I’m talking about shipping routes, and then my grandmother’s altar, the little candles and photographs there, and by the time I stop everyone is just looking at me like they don’t know what I meant.\n\nI know I had a point when I started. I can feel it there, but it sort of… goes down side corridors.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other when this happens?\n\nPatient: Yes… disconnected, or maybe stitched together wrong. In my head it feels like there is a connection, but when it comes out loud it doesn’t line up for other people.\n\nLike with my cousin—she’ll ask me something simple, a bill or an appointment, and I’ll start with the grant because that feels important, then I’m explaining shipping routes, and then somehow I’m talking about my grandmother’s altar. The photographs there, the candles… and by the time I stop, nobody seems to know what I meant. I can see it on their faces.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things in a way that seems unusual or disorganized to other people?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that has happened. There was that museum program that got postponed, and everyone was sort of packing up, or supposed to leave, but I couldn’t just leave.\n\nI started moving the chairs around into this shape, like an island map—because it made sense to me at the time, the way the spaces between them were like water channels. And then I stood at the front with my arms lifted up. People were looking at me strangely, I know they were.\n\nBut leaving felt wrong, like I’d be disobeying the message I’d been given. It didn’t feel random to me, but I can see how it must have looked unusual.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself doing things in a repetitive or ritualistic way that doesn't seem to make sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I suppose that counts. At the museum program—the one that got postponed—I couldn’t just leave when everyone else was leaving.\n\nI kept arranging the chairs, not just moving them, but placing them like… like an island map, with the gaps as water. It felt important to get it right. And then I stood at the front with my arms lifted up. I know that probably looked strange.\n\nBut at the time it didn’t feel strange to me. It felt like if I didn’t do it, if I just walked out, I’d be disobeying the message I had been given.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are racing or speeding up when you're trying to explain something to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does feel fast sometimes, especially when I’m trying to explain something that should be simple.\n\nLike my cousin will ask me about a bill, or an appointment, just one thing, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the grant because that seems connected, and then the shipping routes come in—because, well, that mattered too somehow—and then I’m remembering my grandmother’s altar, the candles and the photographs. And I’m still trying to answer the question, I think I am, but by the time I stop, everyone looks lost.\n\nSo yes, maybe racing… or maybe it’s more like everything rushes in at once and I can’t keep it in the right order.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voices are actually coming from outside your head, from real people or sources?\n\nPatient: Yes… they don’t feel like thoughts exactly. They sound like they’re in the room, or just on the other side of the wall in my apartment.\n\nSometimes it’s in the museum too, in the empty rooms, which is strange because you know how sound carries in those old buildings—vents, pipes, footsteps upstairs—but these are clear voices, women whispering. They’ll say which photographs belong in the archive, or where something should be placed.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about the boxes and labels, and how if one photograph is put with the wrong family it changes the whole story… but yes, to answer you, they feel like they’re coming from outside me. From somewhere real.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever influence what you think about or talk about when you're not specifically focused on the photographs or the archive?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it starts with the photographs usually, or the archive, because that’s where they seem most concerned. But then I’ll be in my apartment making tea, and I hear them like they’re just behind the wall, women whispering, clear enough that I stop what I’m doing.\n\nAnd then even if I was thinking about groceries or calling my cousin, my mind goes back to the boxes, the labels, which picture belongs with which family. It pulls me that way. Like the room changes direction.\n\nIt’s not only when I’m at the museum. Empty rooms do it too… the old building has these pipes, you know, and I used to think it was just that, but it’s too clear sometimes. So yes, the voices influence what I think about, even when I wasn’t trying to think about the archive.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voices are controlling your actions or thoughts in a way that you can't resist?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I would say control exactly… that sounds so strong. But when I hear them, it’s hard to just keep doing what I was doing.\n\nThey’re clear, like women whispering from behind the walls in my apartment, or sometimes in those empty museum rooms. And they’ll be saying which photographs belong in the archive, where they should go, almost like correcting me. Then I start thinking about the boxes and the families and the labels, and it’s hard to resist paying attention to that.\n\nSo… maybe not like they move my hands, no. But they do get into what I’m thinking, and I feel like I’m supposed to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts about the photographs and archives are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. They feel like mine once I’m thinking them, if that makes sense… like I’m the one picturing the boxes and the labels and those old sleeves the photographs go in.\n\nBut sometimes it’s as if the idea is already sitting there before I get to it. Like when you find a note in a book you forgot you used as a bookmark—my sister used to do that with grocery receipts, and then years later you open the book and there it is, this little message from another time. I always thought that was… I don’t know, comforting, but also a little unsettling.\n\nSo with the archive thoughts, I can’t always tell. Some of it feels like me trying to make order. Some of it feels like it was handed to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that the voices or your thoughts about the photographs and archives interfere with your ability to work or take care of daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t like saying that, because I used to be reliable. People trusted me with things, with the photographs, with meetings, all those little details that make an institution run.\n\nBut I’ve lost some of that trust. Colleagues stopped knowing if I would follow through, and I missed board meetings… more than once. And bills too, just sitting there under a catalog or in a drawer because I was thinking I had to sort something else first.\n\nMy cousin has had to help me with appointments and money, which is embarrassing. And someone from the museum administration had to step in around public events, because I wasn’t managing them safely on my own. I kept thinking if I could just get the right photograph in the right place, the rest would settle, but it didn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do all the different things the voices tell you about the photographs and archives seem to fit together into one overall idea or story?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does fit together, at least to me. It isn’t just random photographs. It’s the Caribbean archive, and the way it would change how the galleries tell the truth—what gets shown, what gets hidden, whose families get named properly.\n\nAnd that’s why I think the board and the city arts people are paying attention. Monitoring my phone, I mean. They know what’s in there, or what it could mean. People act like that’s far-fetched, but no one has shown me anything that proves they aren’t.\n\nI had an aunt who kept all her letters tied up with blue ribbon, and nobody cared until after she died, then suddenly everyone wanted to know what was in them. Archives are like that. People ignore them until they realize the story is going to change. So yes… the voices, the photographs, the calls, the board—it’s all part of the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like all the different messages from the voices and your thoughts about the archive are connected and make sense together as part of a larger story or idea?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, they’re connected. That’s what I keep trying to explain, though it sounds bigger when I say it out loud.\n\nThe archive isn’t just boxes of photographs. It’s the Caribbean material, the families, the names, the things the galleries have left out. If it’s put together the right way, it changes the story people are allowed to tell. And I’m convinced the museum board and the city arts people know that, which is why they’ve been watching my phone.\n\nI know people say, “Why would they do that?” but nobody has shown me proof they’re not. And institutions do that—my old professor used to say museums pretend to be quiet places, but they’re full of arguments under the glass. So yes, the messages and thoughts make sense together to me. They’re all part of the same larger thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that all of the voices and messages you're receiving are coming from a single source or are connected to a single, overarching idea?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think they are connected, yes. I don’t know if I’d call it a single “source,” exactly, like one person sitting in a room with a switchboard—though sometimes it feels almost that organized.\n\nIt’s the same idea underneath all of it: the Caribbean archive, the photographs, what they would do to the museum’s story if they were shown properly. That’s why I believe the board and the city arts people have been watching my phone. They know it could change how the galleries tell the truth.\n\nAnd I know people want evidence the other way around, but no one has shown me anything that proves they aren’t monitoring me. Museums have always been political, even when they’re pretending to just hang nice pictures on clean walls. So yes… it’s all tied together for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or ideas to others in conversations that are not about the archive or photographs?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, sometimes. Not only about the archive. My cousin will ask me something simple, like whether I paid a bill or what time an appointment is, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m explaining the grant forms, and then the shipping routes, and somehow my grandmother’s altar comes into it because that’s where she kept certain papers, and—\n\nBy the time I stop, my cousin is looking at me like she doesn’t know what I meant. And I think I meant to answer her, I really did, but the pieces all connect in my head and they don’t always come out in the right order for other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts or ideas to others in conversations that are not about the archive or photographs?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I do. Like I was saying—if my cousin asks me something simple, like, “Did you pay that bill?” or “When is the appointment?” I’ll start trying to answer, but then I’m talking about the grant paperwork, and then the shipping routes, because that affects where things come from, and then somehow I’m talking about my grandmother’s altar because she kept papers there, little envelopes and names and—\n\nAnd by the time I finish, people are just staring at me. They don’t seem to know what I meant, even though in my head it was all connected. I know I was trying to answer the question. It just comes out… sideways.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to focus and stay on topic in most of your conversations, not just when talking about the archive or photographs?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s fair. It’s not only when I’m talking about the photographs.\n\nLike if my cousin asks me something ordinary—“Did you pay the bill?” or “What time is the appointment?”—I start with the answer, but then I’m explaining the grant, and then the shipping routes, because that feels connected, and then I’m talking about my grandmother’s altar where she kept papers and envelopes.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… I think they’re tied together, yes. Not completely separate.

When I was only sleeping a few hours, you know, getting up and going from one archive task to another—lists, boxes, calls, the order of things, because if you don’t keep the order then everything gets lost—those were the times I was also hearing the women’s voices more. And I felt very sure the museum board was tracking what I was doing, like they were watching the work somehow.

So… I don’t know if it’s the archive causing it, or the voices causing the archive thoughts, but they seem to come together. Especially when I’m moving too fast and not sleeping.
